```text
201 (ii) DIAGNOSIS: we keep giving ONE INTERPRETATION AFTER ANOTHER
        interpretation = SUBSTITUTING ONE EXPRESSION OF THE RULE FOR ANOTHER
        -> the regress is STRUCTURAL, so the first step was the mistake
   |
   v
201 (iii) RESOLUTION: a way of grasping a rule WHICH IS NOT AN INTERPRETATION,
        exhibited in OBEYING and GOING AGAINST it in actual cases
   |
   +--> 202  obeying a rule is a PRACTICE; thinking one obeys is not obeying
   |          -> HENCE NO PRIVATE RULE-FOLLOWING   [premise of limb 3]
   +--> 217  justifications exhausted -> 'This is simply what I do'
   |
   v
GUARD: NORMATIVITY != REGULARITY.  practice has MISTAKES and STANDARDS;
   regularity has exceptions and frequencies.  Community behaviour is NOT
   the criterion, or 'correct' collapses into 'usual'
   |
   v
KRIPKE 1982: sceptical paradox + sceptical SOLUTION (assertion-conditions +
   community).  'as it struck Kripke' = Kripkenstein.  Baker/Hacker and
   McDowell: dissolution, not concession.  RULE: valuable, exegetically doubtful
   |
   v
ANSWER LINE: rules do not contain their applications; trained practices do
```

### SESSION 8 — THE CRITIQUE OF PRIVATE LANGUAGE: THE TARGET, THE DIARY "S", AND THE BEETLE

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Could you invent a word for a feeling that only you can have, teach it to nobody, and use it correctly in a diary? It sounds obviously possible. Wittgenstein argues that it is not — not because you would be watched, and not because feelings are unreal, but because you would have given yourself no way of being **wrong**, and a sign you cannot misuse is not yet a sign you are using.

**Technical definition:** A **private language**, in the sense **§243** defines and **§244–271** examine, is a language whose *"individual words refer to what can only be known to the person speaking; to his immediate private sensations"*, so that *"another person cannot understand the language."* The words are given meaning by **private ostensive definition** — attending to a sensation and associating a sign with it — and the sensations are **logically** private, not merely undisclosed. The critique argues that such a definition establishes no **criterion of correctness**, so that no distinction can arise between applying the sign correctly and merely **seeming** to apply it correctly; where that distinction cannot arise, *"we can't talk about 'right'"* (**§258**), and a sign with no possible misuse has no use.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The private linguist is not accused of insincerity, of solitude or of having no inner life; he is shown to have equipped himself with no way of being wrong — and since a sign that cannot be misapplied has not yet been applied, his diary records nothing at all.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **logically private, not merely secret** — the target, stated so the whole answer is on the right object
- **private ostensive definition (§258)** — the exact mechanism under attack
- **criterion of correctness** — the missing element, in three words
- **"whatever is going to seem right to me is right" (§258)** — quoted, with its conclusion attached
- **the beetle-in-a-box (§293)** — *"drops out of consideration as irrelevant"*
- **§202: no rule can be obeyed privately** — the imported premise

**How to use them:** spend the **first two sentences on the target**, because at least half the marks in the 2020 and 2024 parts are lost by candidates who attack a secret code; give the five-step core as a numbered argument rather than as prose; and place the **beetle** *after* the diary, since it removes a residual temptation rather than doing the primary work.

#### Plain-language visual — four things that are NOT the target

```text
   WHAT WITTGENSTEIN IS *NOT* ATTACKING
   ==========================================================================
   1 A SECRET CODE
       Two spies share a cipher nobody else can read. -> This is an
       ENCRYPTED PUBLIC LANGUAGE. It is decipherable IN PRINCIPLE, and its
       words already have public meanings. NOT the target.
   2 TALKING TO ONESELF
       Muttering, silent rehearsal, an inner monologue. -> Ordinary use of a
       PUBLIC language, performed alone. NOT the target.
   3 HAVING PRIVATE THOUGHTS OR FEELINGS
       Undisclosed grief, an unconfessed hope, a pain nobody notices.
       -> Wittgenstein never denies these. NOT the target.
   4 A SOLITARY SPEAKER  (Crusoe, a lone survivor, a child raised alone)
       -> A person alone may still use a language with PUBLIC-TYPE criteria:
       stable techniques, repeatable circumstances, checkable applications.
       Physical solitude is NOT logical privacy. NOT (straightforwardly)
       the target - see the objection in section 6 below.
   ==========================================================================
   WHAT *IS* THE TARGET  (243)
       A language whose words are given meaning by attending to a sensation
       that ONLY THE SPEAKER CAN KNOW, so that another person COULD NOT IN
       PRINCIPLE understand it - not even if told everything.
       The words would be defined by PRIVATE OSTENSION alone, and would
       therefore carry their standards of correct use nowhere but in the
       speaker's own present impression.
   ==========================================================================
   WHY THE DISTINCTION IS WORTH HALF THE MARKS
       Every one of the four non-targets is REFUTED BY EXAMPLE in a
       sentence. If your script attacks one of them, the examiner sees an
       answer to a question that was not asked.
```

**In simple words:** imagine trying to check the time by remembering what the clock looked like — and having no other clock, no timetable, no sunrise, nothing. Whatever you remember will feel right, because there is nothing to feel wrong against. Wittgenstein's image at §265 is exactly this: buying several copies of the morning paper to assure yourself that what it says is true.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Logical**, not contingent, privacy | in principle unteachable, not merely undisclosed |
| The attack is on **private ostensive definition** | not on sensation, not on solitude |
| The missing item is a **criterion of correctness** | a standard independent of the present impression |
| §258's conclusion is about **"right"**, not about "S" alone | *"here we can't talk about 'right'"* |
| The beetle is a **supplementary** move | it removes the private object's relevance to the shared word |

> **One-line memory:** where nothing could count as getting it wrong, nothing counts as getting it right — and where nothing counts as getting it right, no rule has been given.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the five-step core, the diary, and the beetle

```text
   THE LOGICAL CORE - WRITE THIS AS A NUMBERED ARGUMENT
   ==========================================================================
   1  A meaningful sign requires a RULE OF USE: a standard by which its
      application counts as correct or incorrect.
   2  A rule of use requires a CRITERION OF CORRECTNESS independent of the
      user's mere present seeming.                       [PI 202 supplies
                                                          this: obeying a
                                                          rule is a practice]
   3  A purely private 'rule' has NO such independent criterion: the subject
      is sole judge, and whatever seems right to him IS right.       [258]
   4  Therefore a purely private 'rule' is not a rule.
   5  Therefore a private language - one whose words get their meaning by
      private ostensive definition - is IMPOSSIBLE.
   ==========================================================================
   THE DIARY ARGUMENT  (258, with 260, 265, 270)
     SET-UP  'I want to keep a diary about the recurrence of a certain
             sensation. To this end I associate it with the sign "S" and
             write this sign in a calendar for every day on which I have
             the sensation.'
     STEP 1  No definition can be formulated. -> 'But still I can give
             myself a kind of OSTENSIVE definition' - by concentrating my
             attention on the sensation and, as it were, pointing inwardly.
     STEP 2  What is that ceremony for? 'It serves to establish the
             connexion between the sign and the sensation.' But 'I impress
             it on myself' can only mean: this process brings it about that
             I REMEMBER the connexion RIGHT in future.
     STEP 3  And here we have no criterion of correctness. 'One would like
             to say: whatever is going to seem right to me is right. And
             that only means that HERE WE CAN'T TALK ABOUT "RIGHT".'
     260     'Well, I BELIEVE that this is the sensation S again.' -
             Perhaps you believe that you believe it.
     265     The memory-check compared to buying SEVERAL COPIES OF THE
             MORNING PAPER to assure oneself that what it says is true.
             Also: a table that exists only in the imagination is not a
             table - 'justification consists in appealing to something
             INDEPENDENT'.
     270     THE INSTRUCTIVE VARIANT: suppose writing 'S' turns out to
             coincide with a rise in blood pressure that a manometer
             independently registers. Now the sign has a USE - and note
             that whether I identified the sensation RIGHTLY has become
             irrelevant. A public criterion did the work.
   ==========================================================================
   THE BEETLE-IN-A-BOX  (293)
     'Suppose everyone had a box with something in it: we call it a
      "beetle". No one can look into anyone else's box, and everyone says
      he knows what a beetle is only by looking at HIS beetle.'
     * The thing in the box might be different for each person.
     * It might even be constantly changing.
     * The box might be EMPTY.
     CONCLUSION  'If we construe the grammar of the expression of sensation
      on the model of "object and designation" the object DROPS OUT OF
      CONSIDERATION AS IRRELEVANT.'
   ==========================================================================
   WHAT THE BEETLE DOES AND DOES NOT SHOW
     DOES     the grammar of a SHARED sensation-word cannot be fixed by an
              item to which only one speaker has access
     DOES NOT prove sensations do not exist; deny that people feel
              different things; or show that inner differences never matter
     NOTE     293 is explicitly conditional: IF we construe the grammar on
              the object-and-designation model. The beetle attacks a MODEL.
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Three of the seven routed parts (2019, 2020, 2024) are answered from this diagram. The 2024 part wants steps 1–5 with the target correctly identified; the 2020 part quotes §243's own question and wants the diary worked through with the memory objection answered; the 2019 part wants steps 1–5 turned on solipsism, which is Session 10.

#### 1. §243: the question, exactly as Wittgenstein asks it ✅

✅ **§243** is the remark the 2020 question quotes. It begins with a human being encouraging himself, giving himself orders, obeying, blaming and punishing himself — and observes that we could imagine human beings who spoke *only* in monologue. A researcher could learn their language, because it accompanies their ordinary activities. Then comes the real question: *"But could we also imagine a language in which a person could write down or give vocal expression to his inner experiences — his feelings, moods and the rest — for his private use?"* And the immediate clarification: *"the individual words of this language are to refer to what can only be known to the person speaking; to his immediate private sensations. So another person cannot understand the language."*

⚠️ **The structure of §243 is itself an argument, and it is regularly missed.** The monologuists are introduced precisely to be **set aside**: their language is *not* private in the relevant sense, because it is our language used alone. The question is then narrowed to the case where the words could not be understood by another **in principle**. ⚠️ A candidate who begins the 2020 answer by reproducing this narrowing has already secured the target-identification mark that most scripts lose.

#### 2. The diary argument, step by step ✅

✅ **§258.** The set-up, the inward pointing, the demand for a criterion, and the collapse are given in the diagram above. Three refinements deserve separate statement, because they are where the argument is usually attacked:

**(a) It is not an argument from fallible memory.** ⚠️ The objection "but memory is unreliable, and public memory is unreliable too" misses the point. Wittgenstein's claim is not that private memory is *worse* than public memory; it is that in the private case there is **nothing for memory to be reliable or unreliable about**. In the public case, a misremembering can be corrected by a chart, a second speaker, a written record. In the private case the only available check is a further deliverance of the same faculty, which is why §265 compares it to buying several copies of the same newspaper.

**(b) "Right" is what collapses, not "S".** ⚠️ The conclusion of §258 is carefully worded: not "S is meaningless" but *"here we can't talk about 'right'"*. That is the stronger and more interesting claim, and it is what makes the argument an argument about **rules** rather than about **sensations**. Because the distinction between correct and incorrect cannot arise, the notion of a rule for "S" has no application; and by §202, where there is no rule there is no meaning.

**(c) §270 is the constructive half, and it is regularly omitted.** ✅ Wittgenstein imagines that writing "S" turns out to correlate with a rise in blood pressure detectable by a manometer. Now "S" is genuinely useful — and he notes that *whether the diarist recognises the sensation correctly makes no difference*. ⚠️ This is not a concession; it is the diagnosis completed. As soon as the sign acquires a use, the use is supplied by something **public**, and the supposed private identification does no work at all. An answer that includes §270 shows that the argument has a positive result and not merely a negative one.

#### 3. The beetle, and the exact scope of its conclusion ✅

✅ **§293** starts from the tempting thought: *"if I say of myself that it is only from my own case that I know what the word 'pain' means — must I not say the same of other people too?"* The box example follows, and the conclusion is expressly conditional: *"if we construe the grammar of the expression of sensation on the model of 'object and designation' the object drops out of consideration as irrelevant."*

⚠️ **Three precision points.**
1. The three variations (different in each box, constantly changing, box empty) are not sceptical hypotheses; they are a **demonstration of irrelevance**. If all three are compatible with the word's working exactly as it does, the object cannot be what makes it work.
2. The target is the **object-and-designation model**, not the reality of sensations. §293's own next remark warns that *"that is to say: if we construe the grammar … on the model"* — the conditional is doing the work.
3. The beetle is about a **shared** word. It shows that the private item cannot fix the grammar of a word we all use. It is therefore a supplement to §258, which shows that the private item cannot fix the grammar even for the person who has it.

#### 4. Why this is the third printed limb and not an appendix ⚠️

⚠️ The syllabus prints *Critique of Private Language* as a limb in its own right, and the reason should be stated in any answer: the argument is where the account of meaning developed in Sessions 2–7 is turned on the **one domain that had seemed exempt**. The referential model had been driven out of "the", "if", "five" and "game"; sensation-words were its last refuge, because here it really does look as if a word is attached to an object by inner ostension. §258 closes that refuge, and with it the empiricist and Cartesian projects that made the inner the foundation of the outer.

| The target | What survives the attack |
|---|---|
| **A private-inner foundation for meaning** | An inner life; sensations; first-person expression |
| **Classical empiricist foundationalism** — private sense-data as the ultimate given | Perception, evidence, and the ordinary use of "looks", "seems", "feels" |
| **The referential model for sensation words** | "Pain" as a word with a rich public grammar (Session 9) |
| **Cartesian privileged access as a semantic foundation** | Asymmetry between first and third person, redescribed as **avowal** (§244) |

#### 5. The five-step core, written out for the exam ⚠️

```text
   1  A sign has meaning only if there is a rule for its use.
   2  A rule for its use requires a criterion of correct application,
      independent of how things presently seem to the user.        [202]
   3  A private ostensive definition supplies no such criterion:
      the only available check is a further seeming of the same kind. [258, 265]
   4  Hence, in the private case, 'correct' and 'seems correct' coincide,
      and where they coincide, 'correct' has no application.        [258]
   5  Hence there is no rule, hence no meaning, hence no private language.
   ILLUSTRATION, NOT PREMISE  the beetle (293): a private object could vary,
      change or be absent without disturbing the public word, so it is not
      what gives the word its grammar.
```

#### 6. Objections, replies and what genuinely remains open ❓

| Objection | Reply | Residue |
|---|---|---|
| **The Crusoe / solitary speaker objection** (associated with Ayer's discussion of the argument): a man alone from birth could invent words for his sensations, and checking memory against memory is no worse than checking it against a community | The target is **logical** privacy, not solitude. A solitary speaker may use **public-type** criteria — regular circumstances, repeatable behaviour, physical correlates, a stable technique — and Wittgenstein's argument attacks only the case in which *no* such criterion is available even in principle | ❓ Whether an actual community is required, or only in-principle shareable standards, is genuinely disputed; the optional block records the readings |
| **The memory objection:** public checks also rely on memory, so the argument proves too much | Public checks rely on memory **plus** something independent of the present impression — a chart, a second person, a record. The private case has only the impression. §265: several copies of the same newspaper | ⚠️ Critics reply that the "independent" check is itself accepted on the basis of memory; the debate is unresolved, and saying so is better than pretending it is not |
| **The behaviourism charge:** if "pain" gets its meaning from public use, "pain" means pain-behaviour | Answered in Session 9 by **criteria against symptoms** and by §304, 307–308 | ⚠️ Whether criterial grammar fully respects inner phenomenology remains contested |
| **The phenomenological objection:** the argument makes the felt quality of sensation irrelevant | §293 attacks a **model** of the grammar, not the felt quality; §304 insists the sensation is "not a something, but not a nothing either" | ❓ Whether that formula is a solution or a refusal to answer is a live question |

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Wittgenstein's answer to the question whether we could imagine a language for a person's inner experiences, for his private use, is that we could not — because such a language would have to be founded on private ostensive definition, and private ostensive definition establishes no criterion of correctness, so no rule and therefore no meaning is created.
> **Named evidence.** §243 first sets aside the case that is not at issue, imagining human beings who speak only in monologue and noting that a researcher could learn their language, before narrowing the question to words that "refer to what can only be known to the person speaking; to his immediate private sensations", so that "another person cannot understand the language". §258 then works the case: the diarist associates "S" with a recurring sensation and gives himself a kind of ostensive definition by concentrating his attention, but the ceremony can only mean that it brings it about that he remembers the connection rightly in future — "and here we have no criterion of correctness … whatever is going to seem right to me is right. And that only means that here we can't talk about 'right'." §265 compares the internal check to buying several copies of the morning paper to assure oneself of its truth, and §202 has already established that obeying a rule is a practice, so that thinking one obeys is not obeying.
> **Analysis.** The argument is therefore about rules and not about sensations, and its conclusion is that the distinction between correct and seemingly correct cannot arise in the private case — where that distinction cannot arise, "correct" has no application, and a sign whose application cannot be wrong has not been given a use. §270 completes the diagnosis constructively: once "S" is correlated with a manometer reading it acquires a genuine use, and Wittgenstein observes that whether the diarist identified the sensation rightly has now become irrelevant, which shows that whatever work the sign does is done by the public criterion. §293's beetle supplements this by showing that a private item which might differ in every box, change constantly, or be absent altogether cannot be what fixes the grammar of a shared sensation-word.
> **Qualification.** The argument's target must be stated precisely or it collapses into a much weaker claim: it is not directed at secret codes, which are decipherable in principle, at talking to oneself, at unshared thoughts, or at physical solitude, since a solitary speaker may still employ public-type criteria. Nor does it deny that sensations exist or that they have a felt character; §304 insists that the sensation is "not a something, but not a nothing either", and §293's conclusion is expressly conditional on construing the grammar of sensation on the model of object and designation. What remains genuinely open is whether an actual community is required rather than merely in-principle shareable standards, and whether the appeal to independent checks can be sustained without itself relying on memory.

#### Exam link

- **2024 Q1(d)** (10 marks) — *"Why does later Wittgenstein think that there cannot be a language that only one person can speak — a language that is essentially private?"* The target paragraph, the five-step core, the beetle in one sentence, and the solitary-speaker qualification. That is the whole answer.
- **2020 Q4(c)** (15 marks) — the question quotes §243 itself. Reproduce the printed quotation, narrow the target as §243 does, then work §258 with §260, 265 and 270, then handle the memory and Crusoe objections.
- **2019 Q3(b)** (15 marks) — Session 10 applies this apparatus to solipsism.
- **2025 Q3(a)** (20 marks) — the private-language critique is the terminus of the transition: it is where the abandonment of the referential model finally reaches the inner domain that model had seemed to fit best.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Private language = a secret code" | A code is a **public** language encrypted; it is decipherable in principle |
| "The argument shows sensations do not exist" | §304: *"not a something, but not a nothing either"*; §293 attacks a **model** |
| "It shows that a solitary person cannot have a language" | It targets **logical** privacy; solitude may still use public-type criteria |
| "It is an argument about unreliable memory" | It is an argument about the **absence of an independent standard** |
| Beetle first, diary second | The diary carries the argument; the beetle removes a residual temptation |
| Omitting §270 | §270 shows what happens when a **public** criterion is supplied — the diagnosis completed |

#### Revision notes

- **§243** monologuists set aside → the real question: words referring to *what can only be known to the person speaking*, so that **another person cannot understand the language**.
- **§258** diary "S"; inward ostension; no criterion of correctness; *"whatever is going to seem right to me is right"* → *"here we can't talk about 'right'"*.
- **§260** "I believe that this is the sensation S again" — perhaps you believe that you believe it.
- **§265** several copies of the **same newspaper**; an imagined table is not a table; justification appeals to something **independent**.
- **§270** the manometer variant: the sign acquires a use, and correct **identification becomes irrelevant**.
- **§293** beetle: might differ, might change, box might be **empty** → on the object-and-designation model the object **drops out as irrelevant**.
- **§202** obeying a rule is a **practice**; hence **no private rule-following** — the imported premise.
- Four non-targets: secret code · talking to oneself · private thoughts · physical solitude.
- Five-step core: rule → criterion → none available privately → "correct" has no application → no language.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — THE PRIVATE-LANGUAGE ARGUMENT

```text
     BUT a language whose words refer to WHAT ONLY THE SPEAKER CAN KNOW
     -> another person COULD NOT IN PRINCIPLE understand it

MECHANISM UNDER ATTACK: PRIVATE OSTENSIVE DEFINITION - attend to the

   +--> 265  several copies of the SAME newspaper; an imagined table is no
```

### SESSION 9 — SENSATION LANGUAGE: CRITERIA AGAINST SYMPTOMS, §580, AVOWALS, AND THE BEHAVIOURISM CHARGE

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** If "pain" does not get its meaning by naming a private object, where does it get it? From the whole surrounding practice: from the circumstances in which we say it, the behaviour that goes with it, the treatment that follows, the way children are taught. The obvious worry is that this makes pain *equal* pain-behaviour — which is behaviourism, and plainly false. This session is the machinery that blocks that inference.

**Technical definition:** A **criterion (*Kriterium*)** is a **grammatical**, non-inductive ground for applying a concept: it is fixed by the language-game and taught with the word, and it is **defeasible** — it grounds an ascription without entailing it. A **symptom (*Symptom*)** is an **inductive** correlation discovered by experience. **§580** states the governing thesis: *"An 'inner process' stands in need of outward criteria."* **§244** describes how a sensation-word is learned: verbal expressions of pain **replace** crying, so that *"the verbal expression of pain replaces crying and does not describe it"* — the first-person present-tense use is an **avowal (*Äußerung*)** rather than a report. **§354** notes that the grammar itself **fluctuates** between criteria and symptoms. **§304 and 307–308** answer the behaviourism charge: what is denied is the **grammatical fiction** of the inner object, not the inner life.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Wittgenstein is anti-Cartesian without being a behaviourist, and the distinction that makes the position possible is that between a criterion and a symptom: behaviour belongs to the grammar of "pain" without being what "pain" means, because a criterion grounds an ascription without entailing it.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **criterion (*Kriterium*) / symptom (*Symptom*)** — both terms, both glossed
- **defeasible** — the single word that blocks the behaviourist inference
- **"An 'inner process' stands in need of outward criteria" (§580)** — quoted
- **avowal (*Äußerung*) (§244)** — the first-person use, correctly named
- **"not a something, but not a nothing either" (§304)** — the anti-eliminativist line
- **"grammatical fiction" (§307)** — the exact word Wittgenstein concedes

**How to use them:** put **defeasible** in the sentence immediately after you first mention criteria, because that is where the behaviourist objection would otherwise land; quote **§580** as the thesis and **§304** as the guard; and use the **angina** example from the *Blue Book*, since a worked non-psychological case makes the distinction unmistakable in two lines.

#### Plain-language visual — the same two questions, asked about a sore throat

```text
   'HAS HE GOT ANGINA?'          - Blue Book example, Wittgenstein's own
   ==========================================================================
   CRITERION                            SYMPTOM
   the presence of a particular         an inflamed throat
   BACILLUS in the blood
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   Established by  DEFINITION           Established by  EXPERIENCE
   We DEFINED angina as the illness     We FOUND that inflamed throats
   caused by this bacillus              usually accompany it
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   If it failed, we would no longer     If it failed, we would revise a
   know what 'angina' MEANS             generalisation; the concept is
                                        untouched
   ==========================================================================
   NOW THE SAME TWO COLUMNS FOR PAIN
   ==========================================================================
   CRITERION                            SYMPTOM
   pain-behaviour IN APPROPRIATE        a raised pulse; sweating;
   CIRCUMSTANCES - wincing at an        pallor; a groan produced by an
   injury, nursing the limb, saying     ACTOR on a stage
   'it hurts', seeking treatment
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   GRAMMATICAL: it is part of what      INDUCTIVE: a correlation
   'being in pain' comes to; it is      experience has taught us; it can
   how the word is TAUGHT               fail without disturbing the concept
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   DEFEASIBLE: pretence, acting and     DEFEASIBLE TOO - but for a
   suppression are all possible.        different reason: it never
   The criterion GROUNDS without        pretended to be more than evidence
   ENTAILING
   ==========================================================================
   THE ONE SENTENCE THAT DOES THE WORK
     The behaviourist asserts an EQUIVALENCE ('pain' = pain-behaviour).
     Wittgenstein asserts a GRAMMATICAL DEPENDENCE (the concept of pain has
     application only where there are outward criteria for applying it).
     An equivalence is destroyed by one case of successful pretence.
     A grammatical dependence is not.
   ==========================================================================
```

**In simple words:** a criterion is like the rule that tells you what "checkmate" means — you learn what the word means by learning to recognise the position. A symptom is like noticing that people usually sigh when they are checkmated. If people stopped sighing, "checkmate" would be unaffected. If the position stopped counting, "checkmate" would have lost its meaning.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Criterion = **grammatical**, taught with the word | not evidence for a hidden event |
| Symptom = **inductive**, discovered | evidence, and only that |
| Criteria are **defeasible** | they ground without entailing — pretence is possible |
| The grammar **fluctuates** (§354) | criterion and symptom are **roles**, not a fixed taxonomy |
| First person is an **avowal** (§244) | it extends natural expression; it is not self-observation |

> **One-line memory:** behaviour is not what pain *is*; it is what makes the word "pain" a word we can have.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — §580's argument, and the four-move reply to the behaviourism charge

```text
   THE THESIS  (580)   'An "inner process" stands in need of outward criteria.'
   ==========================================================================
   WHAT IT SAYS                          WHAT IT DOES NOT SAY
   The CONCEPT of an inner process       That there are no inner processes
   has application only where there
   are outward criteria for applying it
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   'Remembering', 'intending',           That mental life is nothing but
   'expecting', 'understanding' are      behaviour
   not names of private episodes we
   then report
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   The grammar of psychological words    That I infer my own present pain
   is learned in SHARED practices        from my behaviour - first-person
                                         uses often EXPRESS rather than report
   ==========================================================================
   THE ARGUMENT, NUMBERED
   1 To possess a concept is to master a technique of applying a word
     correctly and incorrectly.                                 [143-202]
   2 Mastery requires circumstances in which application is right and
     circumstances in which it is wrong.
   3 For a putatively 'inner' concept those circumstances cannot themselves
     be further inner items - that restarts the same problem one level down
     (a private criterion for a private criterion).                 [258]
   4 Therefore the circumstances must be OUTWARD: behaviour, situation, what
     precedes and follows - the SURROUNDINGS (Umgebung) of the utterance.
   5 Therefore: an inner process stands in need of outward criteria.  [580]
   ==========================================================================
   THE OBJECTION, RAISED BY WITTGENSTEIN AGAINST HIMSELF          307-308
     'Aren't you at bottom really saying that everything except human
      behaviour is a fiction?'  -  'If I do speak of a fiction, then it is
      of a GRAMMATICAL fiction.'
   ==========================================================================
   THE REPLY IN FOUR MOVES - THE PARAGRAPH THE EXAMINER IS WAITING FOR
   1 THE DENIAL IS OF A PICTURE, NOT A PHENOMENON                      304
     'Not a something, but not a nothing either! The conclusion was only
      that a nothing would serve just as well as a something about which
      nothing could be said.'  What is rejected is the model of sensation
      as an INNER OBJECT privately named - not pain, joy or memory.
   2 THE FIRST FALSE STEP                                              308
     We begin by talking innocently of mental 'processes and states',
     leaving their nature undecided - 'and now the analogy which was to
     make us understand our thoughts falls to pieces. ... THE DECISIVE
     MOVEMENT IN THE CONJURING TRICK HAS BEEN MADE, AND IT WAS THE VERY
     ONE THAT WE THOUGHT QUITE INNOCENT.'
   3 CRITERIA ARE NOT DEFINITIONS
     Because criteria are DEFEASIBLE, 'he is in pain' is NOT EQUIVALENT to
     any statement about behaviour. The behaviourist asserts equivalence;
     Wittgenstein asserts grammatical dependence. STATING THIS DIFFERENCE
     IS WHAT THE EXAMINER IS LOOKING FOR.
   4 THE FIRST/THIRD PERSON ASYMMETRY                              244, 246
     'I am in pain' in the central present-tense case is an AVOWAL
     (Aeusserung): it replaces crying, it does not describe it.
     'He is in pain' uses behavioural and situational criteria.
     246: 'I know I am in pain' is not a piece of knowledge - 'it can't be
     said of me at all (except perhaps as a joke) that I KNOW I am in pain.'
     CAUTION: not every first-person psychological sentence is an avowal
     ('I was afraid last Tuesday' is a report), and neither use identifies
     pain with behaviour.
   ==========================================================================
   THE FLUCTUATION - THE CANDID ADMISSION THAT EARNS A 20-MARK VERDICT  354
     'The fluctuation in grammar between criteria and symptoms makes it look
      as if there were nothing at all but symptoms.'
     What is a criterion in one language-game may be a symptom in another;
     the distinction is a GRAMMATICAL ROLE, not a fixed taxonomy. Say
     whether you regard that as candour about how concepts work, or as an
     admission that the key notion is not sharp - and give a reason.
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Without this session the private-language argument is indefensible: every examiner's first objection to Session 8 is "so you are a behaviourist", and this is the only place the answer lives. Two sentences from here belong in every private-language answer.

#### 1. Where the distinction comes from ✅

✅ The **criterion/symptom** vocabulary is developed in the *Blue Book*, **dictated 1933–35** and **published 1958**. ⚠️ Because these were dictations to students rather than a work prepared for print, cite them as *"in the Blue Book"* rather than *"Wittgenstein wrote"* — the same provenance discipline that applies to the *Investigations* itself.

✅ The **angina** example is Wittgenstein's own: we define angina as the illness produced by a particular bacillus, so the presence of that bacillus is the **criterion**; we discover by experience that an inflamed throat usually accompanies it, so the inflamed throat is a **symptom**. ⚠️ Using a **non-psychological** example first is a deliberate teaching choice and a good exam choice: it shows the distinction is general, and it prevents the reader from suspecting that it was invented to save a doctrine about the mind.

#### 2. Why the distinction is indispensable ⚠️

⚠️ State the necessity explicitly, because it converts a piece of exposition into an argument. Without the distinction, the private-language argument has the following shape: sensation-words get their meaning from public use → public use is behaviour → therefore "pain" means pain-behaviour. That is **logical behaviourism**, and it is refuted by a single successful piece of pretence.

With the distinction, the shape is different: sensation-words get their meaning from a language-game whose criteria are **behaviour in appropriate circumstances** → criteria are grammatical and **defeasible** → therefore "he is in pain" is grounded, but not entailed, by behaviour. Pretence is not a counter-example; it is a possibility the account **predicts**, since a defeasible criterion is precisely one that can be satisfied where the ascription fails.

#### 3. §580, unpacked ✅

✅ **§580:** *"An 'inner process' stands in need of outward criteria."* The five-step argument is in the diagram. Two features of it should be stressed in an answer.

**(a) Step 3 is the load-bearing step**, and it is the private-language argument in miniature: the circumstances that fix the application of an inner concept cannot themselves be further inner items, because a private criterion for a private criterion restarts §258 one level down. ⚠️ Saying this connects Sessions 8 and 9 into one argument and is worth a mark by itself.

**(b) The scare-quotes around "inner process" are doing work.** ⚠️ Wittgenstein is not asserting that there are inner processes which need outward criteria; he is examining the phrase. §305–308 press exactly this: the temptation is to think that denying an inner process is denying that anything happens, and §308 diagnoses that temptation as the result of an earlier, apparently innocent move — treating "mental processes and states" as a category whose nature can be left undecided while we go on to talk about them.

#### 4. Avowals, and the first/third-person asymmetry ✅

✅ **§244** asks how words refer to sensations and answers with a story about **teaching**: a child hurts himself and cries; adults talk to him and teach him exclamations and later sentences; *"they teach the child new pain-behaviour"* — and so *"the verbal expression of pain replaces crying and does not describe it."*

✅ **§246** draws the consequence for the alleged privileged access: *"In what sense are my sensations private? — Well, only I can know whether I am really in pain; another person can only surmise it. — In one way this is false, and in another nonsense."* False, because other people very often do know when I am in pain; nonsense, because *"it can't be said of me at all (except perhaps as a joke) that I know I am in pain."* One does not learn of one's own pain by observation, and where doubt has no place, knowledge has no place either.

⚠️ **Three cautions.**
1. **Not every first-person psychological sentence is an avowal.** "I was frightened last Tuesday" is a **report**, subject to memory and error. The avowal analysis covers the central **present-tense** case.
2. **The asymmetry is a fact about grammar, not a privilege of access.** The Cartesian explains the asymmetry by an inner theatre to which one person has a ticket; Wittgenstein explains it by the different **roles** of the first- and third-person uses.
3. ⚠️ **This is where the position is most contested.** Critics ask whether the avowal analysis can accommodate self-knowledge that is genuinely informative, or the difference between sincerely avowing and being mistaken about one's own state. Wittgenstein has resources — the notion of surroundings, the possibility of a confused avowal — but no settled theory, and an honest answer says so.

#### 5. The fluctuation, and the verdict to give ⚠️❓

✅ **§354:** *"The fluctuation in grammar between criteria and symptoms makes it look as if there were nothing at all but symptoms."* Wittgenstein's example is rain: our criterion for rain is a certain sense-impression, but "it is raining" also functions with a barometer reading, and which counts as the criterion shifts with the game.

⚠️ **The verdict this invites is genuinely two-sided, and a twenty-mark answer should take one side and say why.**
- **Reading A (candour).** The fluctuation is an accurate observation about how concepts work: nothing in the notion of a criterion requires it to be fixed for all time, and treating it as a role rather than a taxonomy is a feature, not a bug.
- **Reading B (instability).** If the distinction between criterion and symptom itself shifts, then the anti-behaviourist firewall is not a firm boundary but a local convenience, and the claim that behaviour is *constitutive* of the concept becomes hard to state in general.
❓ Both readings are defended in the literature. ⚠️ The recommendation in this package is Reading A with a concession: the distinction is a genuine grammatical role and does the anti-behaviourist work in the cases that matter, but Wittgenstein's own remark shows that it cannot be drawn once and for all, so the firewall is secured case by case rather than in advance.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Wittgenstein's account of sensation-language is anti-Cartesian without being behaviourist, because it holds that behaviour in appropriate circumstances is a criterion for a psychological ascription — a grammatical and defeasible ground — rather than what the ascription means.
> **Named evidence.** The *Blue Book*, dictated in 1933–35 and published in 1958, distinguishes the criterion of angina, the presence of a particular bacillus which we have defined as constitutive, from its symptom, the inflamed throat that experience has taught us to expect; the same distinction is applied to pain, where wincing, nursing an injured limb and seeking treatment are criteria while a raised pulse or an actor's groan are at best symptoms. §580 states the thesis that an "inner process" stands in need of outward criteria, and §244 shows how such criteria are acquired: adults teach the child new pain-behaviour, so that the verbal expression of pain replaces crying and does not describe it. §246 adds that "I know I am in pain" can be said of me at all only as a joke, since I do not learn of my pain by observation. §307 records the objection in the interlocutor's own voice — that everything except human behaviour is being made a fiction — and answers that if it is a fiction it is a grammatical fiction, while §304 insists that the sensation is "not a something, but not a nothing either".
> **Analysis.** The distinction is what makes the position a third way rather than a version of behaviourism. A behaviourist asserts an equivalence between psychological ascriptions and behavioural statements, and a single case of successful pretence refutes an equivalence; Wittgenstein asserts a grammatical dependence, on which pretence is not a counter-example but a possibility the account predicts, since a defeasible criterion is exactly one that may be satisfied where the ascription fails. §580's argument is also the private-language argument in miniature, because its load-bearing step is that the circumstances fixing an inner concept cannot themselves be further inner items without restarting §258 one level down; and §308 explains why the difficulty seemed compelling, by locating the decisive movement in the conjuring trick at the apparently innocent step of speaking of mental processes and states whose nature is left undecided.
> **Qualification.** Three limits should be conceded. Not every first-person psychological sentence is an avowal, since past-tense reports are subject to memory and error, so the analysis covers the central present-tense case. §354's admission that the grammar fluctuates between criteria and symptoms means that the distinction is a grammatical role rather than a fixed taxonomy, and cannot be drawn once and for all. And the residual question — whether criterial grammar adequately respects the felt character of experience and the informativeness of self-knowledge — remains genuinely open, which is why §304's formula is read by some as a solution and by others as a refusal to answer.

#### Exam link

- **2020 Q4(c)** (15 marks) and **2024 Q1(d)** (10 marks): the behaviourism objection is the first thing an examiner will test after the diary argument. Two sentences from §304 and 307–308 plus the word **defeasible** answer it.
- **2019 Q3(b)** (15 marks): the first/third-person asymmetry is what allows Wittgenstein to reject solipsism without asserting an inductive argument for other minds.
- **2025 Q3(a)** (20 marks): §244's account of learning "pain" is the clearest single case of meaning displaced from reference to use, and it makes the transition concrete.
- **2023 Q4(c)** (15 marks): the failure of the referential model in its **last refuge** is the strongest of the reasons for the shift.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Wittgenstein is a logical behaviourist" | Criteria are **defeasible**; a behaviourist asserts an **equivalence**, which pretence refutes |
| "Criteria are evidence for a hidden inner event" | They are **grammatical**: the circumstances in which the concept has its home |
| "'Pain' means pain-behaviour" | §304: not a something, but not a nothing either; §307: a **grammatical** fiction |
| "I know I am in pain" treated as knowledge | §246: sayable of me only as a joke; where doubt is excluded, knowledge has no role |
| Treating all first-person uses as avowals | §244 covers the central **present-tense** case; past-tense uses are reports |
| Ignoring §354 | The fluctuation is Wittgenstein's own admission and is where the verdict mark lies |

#### Revision notes

- **Source:** *Blue Book*, dictated **1933–35**, published **1958** — say "in the Blue Book".
- **Angina:** criterion = the **bacillus** (defined); symptom = the **inflamed throat** (discovered).
- **Pain:** criterion = pain-behaviour **in appropriate circumstances**; symptom = raised pulse, an actor's groan.
- **Criteria are defeasible** — pretence, acting and suppression are all possible; they **ground without entailing**.
- **§580** an "inner process" stands in need of **outward criteria**; step 3 = a private criterion for a private criterion restarts §258.
- **§244** the verbal expression of pain **replaces** crying and does not describe it → **avowal (*Äußerung*)**.
- **§246** "I know I am in pain" — sayable only as a joke; no observation, hence no knowledge-claim.
- **§304** *"not a something, but not a nothing either"*; **§307** a **grammatical** fiction; **§308** the decisive movement in the conjuring trick, at the step we thought innocent.
- **§354** the grammar **fluctuates** between criteria and symptoms — take a side on what that shows.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — SENSATION LANGUAGE

```text
PROBLEM: if 'pain' does not name a private object, does it mean BEHAVIOUR?
   |
   v
DISTINCTION (Blue Book, dictated 1933-35, published 1958)
   CRITERION (Kriterium) grammatical · taught with the word · DEFEASIBLE
   SYMPTOM   (Symptom)   inductive   · discovered by experience
   ANGINA: bacillus = criterion | inflamed throat = symptom
   PAIN:   behaviour in appropriate circumstances = criterion |
           raised pulse, an actor's groan = symptom
   |
   v
580  an 'inner process' stands in need of OUTWARD CRITERIA
   1 concept = mastery of a technique  2 mastery needs right/wrong
   3 the circumstances cannot be further INNER items (that restarts 258)
   4 so they are OUTWARD - behaviour, situation, SURROUNDINGS (Umgebung)
   5 hence 580
   |
   v

   2 308  the decisive movement in the conjuring trick - at the innocent step
   3 criteria are DEFEASIBLE -> grammatical dependence, NOT equivalence
   4 244  first person = AVOWAL, replaces crying, does not describe it
          246  'I know I am in pain' - only as a joke
   |
   v
354  the grammar FLUCTUATES between criteria and symptoms
   Reading A candour about concepts | Reading B the firewall is local
   RECOMMENDED: A, with the concession that it is secured case by case
   |
   v
ANSWER LINE: behaviour is not what pain is; it is what makes 'pain' a word
   we can have
```

### SESSION 10 — SOLIPSISM: FROM THE *TRACTATUS* LIMIT TO THE GRAMMATICAL DISSOLUTION

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** The solipsist says: only my own experiences are real; everything else — the world, other people's minds — may be nothing at all. Wittgenstein's later reply is not to prove that other people exist. It is to look at the words the solipsist is using and to ask where he obtained them, on what basis he applies them, and what would count as his applying them wrongly. When those questions are pressed, the thesis loses the vocabulary it needs in order to be stated.

**Technical definition:** **Solipsism** is the position that only the subject's own mind, or its experiences, can be known to exist or is real. The **early** treatment (*Tractatus* 5.6–5.641) holds that *"the limits of my language mean the limits of my world"*, that what the solipsist **means** is quite correct but **cannot be said** — it **shows** itself — and that solipsism strictly carried out coincides with pure realism, the "I" shrinking to an extensionless point. The **later** treatment is entirely different in route: it applies the **private-language** considerations to the solipsist's own vocabulary, showing that a language whose terms are fixed solely by the speaker's present private seeming has no criterion of correctness, so that the thesis is **self-undermining** rather than false. ⚠️ The result is a **dissolution**, not a refutation: no empirical proof of other minds is offered or needed.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The solipsist is not defeated by evidence of other minds but by his own sentence: to say that only his experiences are real he must use words whose application he cannot fix from inside his own experience, so the thesis consumes the language required to state it.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **dissolution, not refutation** — the exact verdict-word, chosen deliberately
- **the limits of my language mean the limits of my world (*Tractatus* 5.6)** — the early formula
- **saying and showing** — how the early book handled it
- **self-undermining** — the later verdict, precisely
- **logical privacy** — what the argument attacks; solitude is not the target
- **no inductive argument for other minds** — what is *not* claimed

**How to use them:** open by distinguishing **refutation** from **dissolution**, since the 2019 verb "culminates" invites a candidate to claim a proof that Wittgenstein never offers; then set out the early treatment briefly and accurately, because getting 5.62 right is the cheapest mark in this answer; and close on what remains unproved, which is where the "critically" content lives.

#### Plain-language visual — three ways to answer a solipsist, and which one is Wittgenstein's

```text
   ROUTE 1  REFUTE HIM  (not Wittgenstein's)
     'Here is one hand, and here is another.'  - Moore's style.
     'Other bodies behave as mine does, so they probably have minds.'
     - the ARGUMENT FROM ANALOGY.
     STATUS: an empirical or quasi-empirical case for other minds. The
     solipsist replies that all the evidence is, once again, HIS experience.
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ROUTE 2  SHOW THAT WHAT HE MEANS CANNOT BE SAID  (Tractatus, 5.62-5.641)
     'The limits of MY language mean the limits of MY world' (5.6).
     'What the solipsist MEANS is quite correct; only it cannot be SAID,
      but makes itself manifest.'
     'The self of solipsism shrinks to a point without extension, and there
      remains the reality co-ordinated with it' - so solipsism strictly
      carried out COINCIDES WITH PURE REALISM.
     STATUS: a limit-of-language treatment via SAYING and SHOWING.
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ROUTE 3  EXAMINE HIS VOCABULARY  (Investigations - the later route)
     Ask: 'only MY experiences are real' - where did you get the words
     'experience', 'real', 'mine', 'only'? On what basis do you apply them?
     What would count as applying them WRONGLY?
     If the answer is 'my present impression', then by 258 correct and
     seems-correct coincide, and the words have no rule.
     If the answer appeals to public-type criteria, the solipsist has
     already conceded the practice he was denying.
     STATUS: DISSOLUTION. The thesis is SELF-UNDERMINING, not false.
   ==========================================================================
   WHICH ROUTE THE 2019 QUESTION WANTS
     'Show how Wittgenstein's critique of solipsism CULMINATES in the
      critique of private language.'
     -> Narrate 2, then 3. The word 'culminates' is asking for the
        DEVELOPMENT from the early limit-treatment to the later grammatical
        one - and for the observation that the ROUTE changed even though
        the anti-solipsist direction did not.
   ==========================================================================
```

**In simple words:** it is like someone announcing, in fluent English, that no language he speaks can be understood by anyone. The claim is not disproved by producing a listener; it is undermined by the fact that he had to use English to make it. The solipsist is in that position with respect to every concept in his thesis.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| The verdict is **dissolution** | not "solipsism is false", and not "other minds are proved" |
| The early route is **saying/showing** | 5.62; the later route is **grammar and criteria** |
| Do not say the later work simply completes the early | ⚠️ the **route** changes; that is the examinable development |
| **Logical** privacy is the target | a solitary survivor is not thereby a solipsist |
| The asymmetry from Session 9 does the work | avowal versus criterial ascription, not inference from behaviour |

> **One-line memory:** you cannot say, in a language you learned from others, that there is nobody but you.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the four-step later argument, and the honest limits of the result

```text
   STEP 1  STATE THE THESIS IN ITS STRONGEST FORM
     'Only my experiences are real. The external world and other minds may
      not exist.'  Note that the solipsist is not merely doubting: he is
      making a claim, and a claim needs concepts with determinate use.
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   STEP 2  ASK WHERE THE CONCEPTS COME FROM
     'experience' · 'real' · 'mine' · 'only' · 'exist' · 'pain' · 'seeing'
     Each is a word with a grammar. Each was learned. Each has criteria of
     application that the solipsist is presently using.
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   STEP 3  APPLY THE PRIVATE-LANGUAGE RESULT                        258, 202
     If the solipsist says the criteria are fixed by his own present
     seeming, then correct and seems-correct coincide -> 'here we can't
     talk about "right"' -> the words have no rule -> the thesis has not
     been stated.
     If he says the criteria are stable, repeatable and checkable, he has
     helped himself to exactly the PUBLIC-TYPE practice his thesis denies.
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   STEP 4  DRAW THE CORRECT VERDICT
     Solipsism is not refuted by evidence; it is SELF-UNDERMINING. The
     attempt to ground all meaning in what only this subject can identify
     removes the conditions under which anything determinate is said.
     COMPARE the Tractatus verdict: strictly carried out, solipsism
     'coincides with pure realism' - the early book also declines to treat
     it as a false empirical thesis.
   ==========================================================================
   WHAT IS **NOT** ESTABLISHED - SAY SO, AND THE ANSWER BECOMES CRITICAL
   X That other minds EXIST (that would need a positive argument this
     method does not offer, and 303: 'just try - in a real case - to doubt
     someone else's fear or pain')
   X That the external world exists (Moore's proof and On Certainty's
     hinges are a different discussion, and On Certainty is enrichment here)
   X That a person could not in fact live alone (physical solitude is not
     logical privacy)
   ==========================================================================
   THE SUPPORTING MATERIAL FROM SESSION 9 THAT BELONGS IN THIS ANSWER
     * 244  'I am in pain' is an AVOWAL - it replaces crying. So the
       first-person case is not a report of a private object that others
       must guess at
     * 246  'only I can know whether I am really in pain' is FALSE in one
       way (others often do know) and NONSENSE in another (I do not learn
       it by observation)
     * 293  the beetle: the private object drops out of the SHARED game
     * 580  an inner process stands in need of outward criteria
     -> Together these dismantle the ASYMMETRY the solipsist relies on,
        without asserting an inductive inference to other minds
   ==========================================================================
```

⚠️ **Caption.** The 2019 part is the only routed question that requires both the early and the later treatment in the same answer. This diagram is its plan: Route 2 briefly, Route 3 in full, then the honest limits.

#### 1. The early treatment, stated accurately ✅

✅ *Tractatus* **5.6:** *"The limits of my language mean the limits of my world."* **5.62:** *"This remark provides the key to the problem, how much truth there is in solipsism. For what the solipsist means is quite correct; only it cannot be said, but makes itself manifest."* **5.64:** solipsism, when its implications are followed out strictly, *"coincides with pure realism"* — the self of solipsism shrinks to a point without extension, and there remains the reality co-ordinated with it.

⚠️ **Two points that must not be lost.** First, the early Wittgenstein does **not** say solipsism is false; he says what the solipsist means cannot be *said*. Second, the mechanism is **saying and showing**, which is the doctrine owned by Topic 06 — so it should be named here in one clause and not expounded. ⚠️ A candidate who writes "the *Tractatus* ignored the self" or "the early Wittgenstein was a solipsist" has made an error that is visible from the section number alone.

#### 2. Why the later route is genuinely different ⚠️

⚠️ It is tempting, and wrong, to present the later argument as the early one completed. The **direction** is the same — solipsism is not a false theory but a confusion — but the **route** is entirely changed:

| | **Early (*Tractatus*)** | **Later (*Investigations*)** |
|---|---|---|
| Where the trouble is located | at the **limit** of language and the world | in the **grammar** of the solipsist's words |
| The operative distinction | **saying** versus **showing** | **criterion** versus **mere seeming** |
| The status of the subject | a **metaphysical** subject at the boundary, not in the world | a **person** among others, with a body, a history and a training |
| Verdict | what he means is correct but **unsayable**; strictly carried out it coincides with realism | the thesis is **self-undermining**: it removes the conditions for saying anything determinate |
| What replaces the doctrine | silence | a **description** of how psychological concepts are actually used |

⚠️ Saying that the route changed is exactly what the 2019 verb "culminates" is testing: a culmination is a development, not a repetition, and the mark is for showing what developed.

#### 3. The argument in full ✅

The four steps are in the diagram. Two of them deserve amplification.

**Step 2 is not a debating trick.** ⚠️ The point is not "you used words, so you lose". It is that the solipsist's thesis is a **claim**, and a claim requires concepts whose application can be right or wrong. So the question "what fixes the application of 'experience' in your mouth?" is the same question §258 asks of "S", and it is legitimate for the same reason.

**Step 3's dilemma is the whole argument, and it should be written as a dilemma.** Either the solipsist's terms are fixed by his own present seeming — in which case, by §258, correct and seems-correct coincide and no rule has been given, so nothing determinate has been asserted; or they are fixed by stable, repeatable, checkable techniques — in which case he has helped himself to precisely the kind of practice his thesis was denying. ⚠️ Presenting it as a dilemma converts the answer from narration into argument and is worth several marks in a fifteen-mark part.

#### 4. What is not proved, and why saying so earns marks ⚠️

⚠️ Three things are **not** established, and a candidate who lists them demonstrates control of the argument's exact strength:
1. **That other minds exist.** No inductive or transcendental proof is offered. §303 makes the relevant observation obliquely: *"Just try — in a real case — to doubt someone else's fear or pain"*, which is a remark about where doubt has application, not an argument that doubt is false.
2. **That the external world exists.** That is Moore's territory and, in a different key, *On Certainty*'s — enrichment here, not doctrine (Session 12).
3. **That solitude is impossible.** Physical solitude is compatible with a language having public-type criteria; the target is **logical** privacy throughout.

⚠️ There is also a genuine objection to record: a determined solipsist may reply that Wittgenstein's argument only shows he cannot *communicate* his thesis, not that it is untrue. The Wittgensteinian answer is that a thesis whose terms have no determinate application has not been formulated at all, so there is nothing left to be true — but this reply depends on the private-language argument's success and inherits whatever residual doubt attaches to it (Session 8, §6).

#### 5. Building the 2019 answer ⚠️

⚠️ A workable five-part plan, which the model answer in the practice section follows:
1. **Thesis:** the critique is a dissolution, and the private-language argument supplies its decisive ground.
2. **Early:** 5.6, 5.62, 5.64 in three lines — limit, saying/showing, coincidence with realism.
3. **Change of route:** the table in §2 above, compressed to two sentences.
4. **The dilemma:** Step 3, stated as a dilemma, with §258 and §202 cited.
5. **Limits and verdict:** no proof of other minds; solitude not the target; §303 on where doubt has application; and the concluding line that the solipsist's thesis consumes its own vocabulary.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Wittgenstein's critique of solipsism culminates in the critique of private language because the later route no longer places solipsism at the limit of what can be said, but examines the solipsist's own vocabulary and shows that it can have no determinate application unless the practice he denies is presupposed.
> **Named evidence.** The *Tractatus* had already declined to treat solipsism as a false empirical thesis: 5.6 states that the limits of my language mean the limits of my world, 5.62 that what the solipsist means is quite correct but cannot be said and makes itself manifest, and 5.64 that solipsism strictly carried out coincides with pure realism, the self shrinking to an extensionless point. The *Investigations* changes the route: §202 establishes that obeying a rule is a practice and that thinking one obeys is not obeying, and §258 shows that a sign whose application is fixed by the user's present seeming admits no distinction between being right and seeming right, so that "here we can't talk about 'right'". §246 removes the asymmetry the solipsist relies upon, since "only I can know whether I am really in pain" is false in one way, because others often do know, and nonsense in another, because I do not learn of my pain by observation; and §244 shows that the first-person utterance replaces crying rather than describing it.
> **Analysis.** The result is a dilemma rather than a refutation. Either the solipsist's terms — "experience", "real", "mine" — are fixed by his own present impression, in which case §258 applies and no rule has been given, so the thesis has not been stated; or they are fixed by stable, repeatable techniques, in which case he has presupposed the very public-type practice his thesis denies. The critique therefore *culminates* in the private-language argument in a precise sense: the early treatment could only say that the solipsist's meaning was unsayable, whereas the later argument identifies exactly what is missing — a criterion of correctness — and shows that its absence is what makes the position unstateable.
> **Qualification.** The verdict must be stated at its true strength. Nothing here proves that other minds exist, and §303's remark that one should try, in a real case, to doubt another's pain concerns where doubt has application rather than establishing an inductive conclusion; nothing here shows that a person could not live in physical solitude, since the target throughout is logical privacy; and a determined solipsist may reply that only the communicability of his thesis has been impugned, to which the Wittgensteinian answer — that a thesis whose terms have no determinate application has not been formulated — inherits whatever residual doubt attaches to the private-language argument itself.

#### Exam link

- **2019 Q3(b)** (15 marks) — the whole session, structured by §5's five-part plan.
- **2020 Q4(c)** (15 marks): the same dilemma, applied to §243's question rather than to the solipsist.
- **2024 Q1(d)** (10 marks): the target-identification paragraph is shared; solitude is not the target in either.
- **2025 Q3(a)** (20 marks): the change of route on solipsism is one of the clearest single illustrations of the transition, and it can be used in a sentence to show the transition was of method rather than of subject.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Wittgenstein refutes solipsism / proves other minds" | It is a **dissolution**; no proof of other minds is offered |
| "The *Tractatus* ignored solipsism" | 5.6–5.641 treat it via **saying and showing**; 5.64: it coincides with pure realism |
| "The later argument simply completes the early one" | The **route** changes — from the limit of language to grammar and criteria |
| "Language is public, therefore other minds exist" | A non-sequitur, and the exact fatal error the directive decoder flags |
| "Solipsism is shown to be false" | It is shown to be **self-undermining** / unstateable |
| Expounding saying and showing at length | That doctrine is **Topic 06**; one clause is the right budget |

#### Revision notes

- *Tractatus* **5.6** limits of my language = limits of my world; **5.62** what the solipsist means is correct but **cannot be said**; **5.64** strictly carried out it **coincides with pure realism**.
- Later route: **grammar and criteria**, not saying/showing.
- **The dilemma:** present seeming → §258, no rule, nothing stated; stable technique → the public-type practice conceded.
- **§246** "only I can know…" — false in one way, nonsense in another; **§244** avowal replaces crying.
- **§303** try, in a real case, to doubt another's pain — a remark about the **place of doubt**.
- Verdict: **self-undermining**, not false; **dissolution**, not refutation.
- Not proved: other minds · the external world · the impossibility of solitude.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — SOLIPSISM

```text
THESIS: 'only my experiences are real'
   |
   +--> EARLY (Tractatus 5.6-5.64): limits of MY language = limits of MY
   |      world | what the solipsist MEANS is correct but CANNOT BE SAID |
   |      strictly carried out it COINCIDES WITH PURE REALISM
   |      MECHANISM: saying / showing            [doctrine owned by Topic 06]
   |
   v
LATER ROUTE CHANGES: from the LIMIT of language to the GRAMMAR of his words
   |
   v
QUESTION: 'experience', 'real', 'mine', 'only' - what fixes their APPLICATION?
   |
   +--> HORN 1  his present seeming -> 258: correct = seems-correct ->
   |      'here we can't talk about "right"' -> no rule -> NOTHING STATED
   +--> HORN 2  stable, repeatable, checkable technique -> he has conceded
   |      the PUBLIC-TYPE practice his thesis denied
   |
   v
SUPPORT FROM SESSION 9: 244 avowal | 246 'only I can know' false and
   nonsense | 293 beetle | 580 outward criteria
   -> the ASYMMETRY the solipsist needs is dismantled WITHOUT an inductive
      argument for other minds
   |
   v
VERDICT: SELF-UNDERMINING - a DISSOLUTION, not a refutation
NOT PROVED: other minds · the external world · that solitude is impossible
   |
   v
ANSWER LINE: you cannot say, in a language you learned from others, that
   there is nobody but you
```

### SESSION 11 — PHILOSOPHY AS THERAPY: LANGUAGE ON HOLIDAY, THE FLY-BOTTLE, AND WHAT "LEAVES EVERYTHING AS IT IS" MEANS

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Wittgenstein thinks most philosophical problems are not questions waiting for answers. They are knots: we take a word out of the setting where it does its work, keep using it as though nothing had changed, and then find ourselves unable to move. The cure is not a new theory but a reminder of how the word actually works — and the sign of success is that the question stops seeming compulsory.

**Technical definition:** The **therapeutic conception of philosophy** holds that philosophical problems arise from **misunderstandings of the grammar of our language** and are to be **dissolved** by **assembling reminders** (§127) and by producing a **perspicuous representation (*übersichtliche Darstellung*)** of the use of our words (§122), rather than by advancing explanatory theories. Its key remarks are **§38** (problems arise *"when language goes on holiday"* — *wenn die Sprache feiert*, closer to *idles*), **§109** (philosophy is *"a battle against the bewitchment of our intelligence by means of language"*; description replaces explanation), **§115** (*"a picture held us captive"*), **§123** (*"a philosophical problem has the form: 'I don't know my way about'"*), **§124** (*"philosophy leaves everything as it is"*), **§128**, **§133** (*"there is not a philosophical method, though there are indeed methods, like different therapies"*), **§255** (*"the philosopher's treatment of a question is like the treatment of an illness"*), and **§309** (*"What is your aim in philosophy? — To show the fly the way out of the fly-bottle"*).

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The therapeutic conception is not a modest retreat from philosophy but a reclassification of its difficulties: a philosophical problem is diagnosed as disorientation rather than ignorance, and the test of success is accordingly not a new proposition established but a compulsion removed.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **language goes on holiday / idles (§38)** — with the German gloss, which is the mark
- **"a picture held us captive" (§115)** — the mechanism named
- **"I don't know my way about" (§123)** — the reclassification of the difficulty
- **perspicuous representation (§122)** — the positive method
- **"leaves everything as it is" (§124)** — quoted, and then correctly bounded
- **"methods, like different therapies" (§133)** — plural, and that plural is doctrine

**How to use them:** give the **§38 German gloss** early — *feiert* is closer to "idles" than to "is on vacation", and the image is machinery running without load — because it is the cheapest single mark in this session; and never quote **§124** without immediately stating what it does and does not commit Wittgenstein to, since the quietism objection is built on that quotation.

#### Plain-language visual — the machine that runs without doing work

```text
   THE MECHANISM OF A PHILOSOPHICAL PROBLEM
   ==========================================================================
   STAGE 1  A WORD AT WORK
     'know', 'time', 'meaning', 'I', 'same', 'exist'
     In its home game the word does a definite job with definite standards.
     'Do you know the way?'  'What time does the train leave?'
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   STAGE 2  THE WORD IS TAKEN OUT OF THE GAME     [38 - language IDLES]
     'But do I really KNOW anything?'  'What IS time?'
     Surface grammar is unchanged - it still looks like a question with an
     answer. But no game has been specified in which it makes a move.
     The engine turns; the wheels are off the ground.
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   STAGE 3  A PICTURE FILLS THE GAP                [115 - held CAPTIVE]
     Something must correspond to the noun. A hidden mechanism of meaning.
     An inner object behind 'pain'. A flow behind 'time'.
     'And we could not get outside it, for it lay in our language and
      language seemed to repeat it to us inexorably.'
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   STAGE 4  DISORIENTATION                          [123 - 'I don't know my
     Not ignorance of a fact.  'I DON'T KNOW MY WAY ABOUT'                way about']
     One is lost in a familiar town, not short of a map of a strange one.
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   STAGE 5  THE TREATMENT                     [122, 127, 133, 255, 309]
     Not a rival theory. ASSEMBLE REMINDERS of how the word is used;
     produce a PERSPICUOUS REPRESENTATION that lets us SEE CONNECTIONS;
     compare the puzzling use with unpuzzling ones.
     'The philosopher's treatment of a question is like the treatment of an
      illness' (255).
     'What is your aim in philosophy? - To show the fly the way out of the
      fly-bottle' (309).
   ==========================================================================
   THE TEST OF SUCCESS  (133, in substance)
     'The real discovery is the one that makes me capable of stopping doing
      philosophy when I want to.'  Not a proposition proved - a COMPULSION
      DISSOLVED.
   ==========================================================================
```

**In simple words:** the fly is not trapped by anything solid. The bottle is open at the top; the fly keeps going towards the light at the sides because that is what flies do. Nothing needs to be broken. Something needs to be *shown*. That is exactly the relation Wittgenstein claims between his reminders and a philosophical problem.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| *feiert* = **idles**, not "vacation" | the image is machinery running without load |
| The picture is **in our language** | §115: which is why it feels inescapable |
| The problem is **disorientation** | §123: not a missing fact |
| Reminders are **assembled**, not invented | §127: the material is already known |
| **Methods**, plural, like therapies | §133: there is no single "Wittgensteinian method" |

> **One-line memory:** the philosopher is not ignorant; he is lost — and a map of a town he already knows is what he needs.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the five load-bearing remarks, and the two objections that must be answered

```text
                             Anscombe's 'holiday' is idiomatic but slightly

   outside it, for it        cannot be escaped by resolving to think

   question is like the
```
